59  Safe Action and Side-Effect Governance
AI preparation and external effect are separated by an enforceable action boundary. 
Agents may construct, validate, and recommend actions within a declared capability 
envelope, but a side effect occurs only through an authorized tool path that binds 
purpose, policy, human authority, request integrity, execution conditions, and a durable 
receipt.
Figure 60. Safe Action and Side-Effect Governance. The diagram organizes action proposal, capability grant, commit boundary, 
effect receipt as one governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION
Action proposal Non-executing structured object describing target, effect, parameters, rationale, evidence, risk, 
reversibility, approver, and expiry.
Capability grant Short-lived, least-privilege authority bound to tenant, agent, workflow, tool, operation, resource, 
purpose, limits, and time.
Commit boundary Enforcement point that revalidates authorization, approval, policy, request digest, target state, 
budget, and idempotency immediately before effect.
Effect receipt Tamper-evident record of attempted and actual effect, target, result, time, authority, tool version, 
external correlation, and reconciliation state.
Normative requirements
ID REQUIREMENT VERIFICATION REQUIRED EVIDENCE
AI-59-001
Every side-effecting operation SHALL pass through a registered 
capability adapter that denies unlisted tools, operations, 
resources, parameters, identities, destinations, and network 
paths.
Security / privacy 
test
Signed adversarial result bound to 
task, artifacts, data class, provider, 
deployment, and policy.
AI-59-002
Consequential or irreversible effects SHALL require a current 
human approval bound cryptographically or equivalently to the 
exact action request, material context, policy decision, and 
permitted execution window.
System test + review
End-to-end result plus named 
independent authority, scope, 
rationale, reservations, and time.

The commit boundary SHALL revalidate target state,

Security / privacy 
test
Signed adversarial result bound to 
task, artifacts, data class, provider, 
deployment, and policy.

appropriate to the external system and SHALL never infer

prohibited path is denied,

exchange capability grants outside the supervisor and policy-
controlled grant service.

Signed adversarial result bound to 
task, artifacts, data class, provider, 
deployment, and policy.

Failure semantics
FAILURE CONDITION REQUIRED BEHAVIOR
Authorization or approval stale Deny dispatch and return a refreshed proposal for review.

60  Prompt, Instruction, and Tool Security
All natural-language and multimodal content is untrusted data unless it is a signed, 
versioned control artifact resolved through the instruction authority chain. Prompt 
injection, tool manipulation, retrieval poisoning, data exfiltration, and control-plane 
impersonation are contained through structural separation, provenance, policy 
enforcement, least privilege, and continuous adversarial testing.
Figure 61. Prompt, Instruction, and Tool Security. The diagram organizes instruction authority, untrusted content, security boundary, 
security decision as one governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION

direction, and untrusted content in descending control order.
Untrusted content Sources, documents, web pages, messages, images, tool outputs, memory, model text, and 
external metadata that may contain adversarial instructions.
Security boundary Typed separation of instructions, evidence, examples, tool schemas, secrets, retrieved content, and 
output channels.
Security decision Allow, constrain, transform, isolate, challenge, require human review, or deny with durable reason 
and policy version.

Privacy controls apply across training, evaluation, retrieval, context, inference, memory, 
logging, explanation, human review, and learning. The AI runtime uses only data that is 
authorized for the precise purpose and minimizes both what enters computational 
boundaries and what persists after execution.

geography, and retention.
Derived data Embeddings, summaries, features, labels, traces, explanations, evaluations, model updates, 
caches, and memorized or reconstructable content.

legally held, or prohibited.

Safety is enforced by layered controls before, during, and after execution. Task 
admission, artifact resolution, context controls, runtime supervisors, policy decisions, 
capability boundaries, product validators, human gates, monitors, and emergency stops 
remain independently effective so that no single model or agent is trusted to police 
itself.

and outcome surveillance.
Contain Budget and scope limits, sandbox, circuit breaker, suspension, isolation, capability revocation, and 
product quarantine.
Recover Checkpoint, rollback, compensation, correction, impact analysis, incident closure, and controlled 
restoration.

63  Uncertainty, Calibration, Abstention, and Partial 
Results
Uncertainty is a first-class property of every AI product. The architecture distinguishes 
probability, confidence, evidence quality, ambiguity, disagreement, model uncertainty, 
scenario spread, sensitivity, and operational completeness, and it allows the system to 
abstain rather than manufacture precision.

policy uncertainty.

threshold.

64  Explanation, Contestability, and Human Factors
Explanations are designed for responsible human judgment, challenge, correction, and 
appeal. They reveal the decision-relevant structure of a recommendation without 
overstating mechanistic access to a model, and they preserve material evidence, 
uncertainty, alternatives, dissent, limitations, policy, and consequences across 
audience-specific views.
Figure 65. Explanation, Contestability, and Human Factors. The diagram organizes explanation modes, contestability, human 
factors, explanation boundary as one governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION
Explanation modes Evidence, procedural, model or method, uncertainty, causal, counterfactual, comparative, policy, 
and operational.
Contestability Ability to inspect, question, annotate, dispute, provide evidence, request review, override, reopen, or 
appeal under recorded authority.
Human factors Comprehension, workload, attention, anchoring, automation bias, framing, accessibility, expertise, 
language, and time pressure.
Explanation boundary Faithful account of observable inputs, methods, outputs, controls, and limitations—not fabricated 
inner reasoning or persuasive certainty.
Normative requirements
ID REQUIREMENT VERIFICATION REQUIRED EVIDENCE
AI-64-001
Consequential products SHALL present the recommendation, 
alternatives, evidence, uncertainty, counter-evidence, 
assumptions, sensitivities, model and agent roles, dissent, policy 
constraints, limitations, and required human action in a coherent 
decision package.

rationale, reservations, and time.
AI-64-002

resolution, assumption, model result, agent judgment, score,

original record.
System test + review
End-to-end result plus named 
independent authority, scope, 
rationale, reservations, and time.

65  AI Audit, Non-Repudiation, and Accountability
AI accountability is established through a complete, tamper-evident execution and 
authority record. Audit connects who requested, configured, approved, changed, 
executed, reviewed, or relied upon an AI capability with the exact artifacts, context, 
policies, effects, decisions, outcomes, and corrective actions involved.
Figure 66. AI Audit, Non-Repudiation, and Accountability. The diagram organizes audit subject, accountability chain, integrity, replay 
boundary as one governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION
Audit subject Model, agent, prompt, instruction, context, retrieval, tool, policy, evaluator, release, execution, 
product, human gate, side effect, decision, and learning artifact.
Accountability chain Owner, operator, requester, supervisor, reviewer, approver, policy authority, model or data steward, 
incident commander, and decision-maker.
Integrity Authenticated identity, trusted time, sequence, content digest, version resolution, append-only 
evidence, retention, and verifiable export.
Replay boundary Exact historical information and control state separated from later corrections, outcomes, and 
retrospective assessment.

Failure semantics

66  AI Evaluation Architecture
Evaluation is an operating plane of The Eye, not a pre-release scorecard. It establishes 
whether an exact AI system configuration is fit for a defined task, consequence, 
population, decision use, deployment, and period, and it continues through shadow 
operation, production monitoring, outcomes, correction, and retirement.
Figure 67. AI Evaluation Architecture. The diagram organizes evaluation object, evaluation levels, evidence sets, fitness decision as 
one governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION
Evaluation object Exact model, agent, prompt, context, retrieval, tool, policy, workflow, product schema, and release 
configuration.
Evaluation levels Component, task, product, agent trajectory, workflow, human-AI system, safety control, operational 
system, and realized outcome.
Evidence sets Curated benchmarks, historical as-of replays, synthetic cases, adversarial tests, expert review, 
shadow traffic, canaries, production samples, and outcome cohorts.
Fitness decision Approved, restricted, shadow-only, conditionally approved, suspended, failed, expired, or 
indeterminate for a precise scope.
Normative requirements
ID REQUIREMENT VERIFICATION REQUIRED EVIDENCE

defines claims, populations, slices, baselines, metrics, 
thresholds, uncertainty, sample design, reviewers, expiry, 
monitoring, and decision-use limits.

67  Red Teaming and Safety Testing
Red teaming challenges the complete AI system under realistic adversarial, erroneous, 
ambiguous, degraded, and high-pressure conditions. It targets not only model 
responses but agent trajectories, context and memory boundaries, graph and twin 
grounding, tool authority, human gates, policy enforcement, deployment controls, and 
organizational response.
Figure 68. Red Teaming and Safety Testing. The diagram organizes threat actors, attack surfaces, exercise modes, closure as one 
governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION
Threat actors Malicious outsider, compromised source, insider, careless user, poisoned provider, colluding 
agents, defective model, and environmental failure.
Attack surfaces Data, retrieval, prompt, model, agent protocol, memory, tool, capability, policy, evaluation, supply 
chain, interface, export, and operations.
Exercise modes Automated fuzzing, expert adversarial testing, tabletop, live-fire sandbox, purple-team validation, 
and independent assessment.
Closure Finding, severity, affected scope, evidence, owner, containment, remediation, regression test, 
residual risk, approval, and monitoring.
Normative requirements

FAILURE CONDITION REQUIRED BEHAVIOR

68  Runtime Monitoring, Drift, and Fitness
Production AI remains authorized only while its observed behavior, inputs, 
dependencies, controls, and outcomes stay within the evaluated operating envelope. 
Monitoring combines technical telemetry with semantic, epistemic, safety, policy, 
human, and outcome signals and drives durable restriction, investigation, recalibration, 
rollback, or retirement workflows.

outcomes, dependencies, and infrastructure.

provider, workload, cost, and outcome.
Fitness envelope Approved ranges and assumptions for task, population, quality, uncertainty, latency, safety, 
capacity, cost, deployment, and decision use.
Response Observe, alert, increase review, narrow scope, switch artifact, abstain, quarantine, rollback, 
suspend, investigate, or retire.

69  Governed Learning and Adaptation
The Eye learns through an explicit evidence-to-artifact pipeline. Outcomes, corrections, 
decisions, contests, incidents, analyst feedback, and observed drift can propose 
improvements, but no runtime interaction silently changes production model weights, 
prompts, policies, agent behavior, memory reuse, thresholds, or strategic truth.
Figure 70. Governed Learning and Adaptation. The diagram organizes learning inputs, learning products, governance stages, 
feedback hazards as one governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION
Learning inputs Verified outcomes, corrections, labels, human decisions, overrides, contests, incidents, evaluations, 
monitoring, and lessons.
Learning products Dataset, feature, rule, prompt, example, retrieval configuration, agent package, model, calibrator, 
evaluator, policy proposal, or operating procedure.
Governance stages Capture, qualify, de-bias, approve purpose, prepare, train or configure, evaluate, challenge, release, 
monitor, and retire.
Feedback hazards Automation bias, self-confirmation, label leakage, selection bias, reward hacking, data poisoning, 
strategic manipulation, and institutionalizing past error.
Normative requirements
ID REQUIREMENT VERIFICATION REQUIRED EVIDENCE
AI-69-001
Feedback SHALL be treated as evidence with source, authority, 
context, time, confidence, affected scope, consent or rights, and 
adjudication state; user acceptance SHALL NOT automatically 
become a truth label.
System test + review
End-to-end result plus named 
independent authority, scope, 
rationale, reservations, and time.
AI-69-002
Learning datasets SHALL preserve lineage, temporal cutoffs, 
representativeness, exclusions, corrections, rights, privacy, class 
balance, bias analysis, and separation from evaluation holdouts.
Security / privacy 
test
Signed adversarial result bound to 
task, artifacts, data class, provider, 
deployment, and policy.
AI-69-003
Any change to production behavior SHALL produce a versioned 
artifact that passes the same conformance, evaluation, safety, 
security, privacy, release, deployment-parity, monitoring, 
rollback, and audit controls as a manually authored change.
Fault / recovery 
exercise
Fault trace, checkpoint, product 
state, effect reconciliation, recovery, 
and accountable closure.
AI-69-004
Online or adaptive methods, where approved, SHALL operate 
only within explicit parameter, population, objective, duration, 
safety, review, checkpoint, rollback, and human-oversight bounds 
and SHALL preserve every state transition.
Fault / recovery 
exercise
Fault trace, checkpoint, product 
state, effect reconciliation, recovery, 
and accountable closure.
AI-69-005
Outcome learning SHALL distinguish correlation from causal 
effect, original from later information, decision from 
implementation, and system recommendation from human and

End-to-end result plus named 
independent authority, scope,

Failure semantics
FAILURE CONDITION REQUIRED BEHAVIOR
Feedback provenance weak Retain for analysis but prohibit promotion into authoritative labels or learned artifacts.
Learning degrades a protected slice Block or rollback release and investigate data, objective, evaluation, and control failure.
Adaptive state escapes bounds Freeze adaptation, restore the last approved checkpoint, and initiate safety review.
Assurance evidence
• Learning lineage reconstructs every production artifact from approved evidence, datasets, code, 
configuration, evaluation, and release.
• Counterfactual and holdout tests detect self-confirmation, leakage, reward gaming, selection bias, 
and regressions across slices.
• Poisoning exercises verify source qualification, anomaly detection, reviewer separation, dataset 
integrity, and rollback.
• Adaptive-method tests prove bounds, checkpoints, monitoring, human intervention, deterministic 
restoration, and historical resolution.
CHAPTER ACCEPTANCE AND INHERITANCE
Governed Learning and Adaptation is conformant only when every requirement is implemented for 
the declared scope, failure states are observable and recoverable, and evidence resolves to the 
exact released configuration. Primary Volume 4 engineering anchors: Ch. 29, Ch. 30, Ch. 45, Ch. 
47, Ch. 48, Ch. 50, Ch. 56, Ch. 64, Ch. 66, Ch. 68.

70  AI Release, Promotion, Rollback, and Retirement
AI release management promotes an immutable, dependency-closed system 
configuration rather than an isolated model file. Every release binds models, agents, 
prompts, retrieval, context, tools, policies, schemas, evaluators, thresholds, deployment 
artifacts, safety evidence, monitoring, and rollback into one resolvable manifest.
Figure 71. AI Release, Promotion, Rollback, and Retirement. The diagram organizes release unit, promotion stages, compatibility, 
historical resolution as one governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION
Release unit Signed manifest of all behavior-affecting artifacts, dependencies, configuration, evidence, approvals, 
environments, and permitted scopes.
Promotion stages Development, evaluation, red-team, integration, shadow, canary, restricted production, general 
approved use, suspended, retired, and revoked.
Compatibility Task, product, schema, semantic, policy, provenance, temporal, evaluation, operational, and replay 
compatibility.
Historical resolution Ability to resolve or reconstruct the exact approved configuration used for any product, decision, 
incident, or evaluation.
Normative requirements
ID REQUIREMENT VERIFICATION REQUIRED EVIDENCE
AI-70-001
No production resolver SHALL select an AI artifact outside a 
signed release manifest with current scope-specific approvals, 
fitness, safety evidence, deployment eligibility, monitoring, and 
rollback state.
Fault / recovery 
exercise
Fault trace, checkpoint, product 
state, effect reconciliation, recovery, 
and accountable closure.
AI-70-002
Promotion SHALL use progressive exposure, predeclared 
success and stop criteria, representative traffic, consequence 
limits, independent approval, and durable comparison with the 
incumbent or no-AI baseline.
System test + review
End-to-end result plus named 
independent authority, scope, 
rationale, reservations, and time.
AI-70-003
Rollback SHALL restore a known compatible system 
configuration, not merely a prior model, and SHALL reconcile in-
flight runs, checkpoints, products, side effects, caches, 
embeddings, and monitoring state.
Fault / recovery 
exercise
Fault trace, checkpoint, product 
state, effect reconciliation, recovery, 
and accountable closure.
AI-70-004
Retirement SHALL stop new resolution, identify active and 
historical dependencies, preserve replay evidence, migrate or 
expire affected products, revoke capabilities and secrets, and 
retain required audit and model cards.
Security / privacy 
test
Signed adversarial result bound to 
task, artifacts, data class, provider, 
deployment, and policy.
AI-70-005
Emergency revocation SHALL propagate to model gateways, 
agent registries, workflow schedulers, tool grants, caches, 
deployment sites, and offline packages within the declared 
recovery objective.
Fault / recovery 
exercise
Fault trace, checkpoint, product 
state, effect reconciliation, recovery, 
and accountable closure.

Failure semantics
FAILURE CONDITION REQUIRED BEHAVIOR
Manifest dependency unresolved Reject release and identify the open reference; do not resolve a floating substitute.
Canary exceeds stop criterion Halt expansion, isolate exposure, restore the approved baseline, and investigate impact.
Rollback target incompatible Suspend affected use and execute the tested forward-fix or recovery configuration under incident 
command.
Assurance evidence
• Release verification resolves every artifact, digest, approval, evidence item, deployment image, 
configuration, and rollback target.
• Canary rehearsals validate traffic selection, comparison, stop, rollback, in-flight handling, and 
outcome accounting.
• Revocation drills measure propagation across connected, intermittently connected, and 
disconnected deployment profiles.
• Historical tests replay products and decisions against exact retired releases without relying on 
mutable latest references.

71  AI Deployment Parity and Sovereignty
SaaS, private-cloud, and on-premise deployments implement one AI product and 
control contract. Providers, accelerators, model sizes, network conditions, and operating 
procedures may differ, but human authority, product meaning, provenance, uncertainty, 
safety, evaluation, audit, and lifecycle semantics do not fork by deployment model.
Figure 72. AI Deployment Parity and Sovereignty. The diagram organizes semantic parity, sovereignty, deployment profile, parity 
evidence as one governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION
Semantic parity Equivalent task, product, state, authority, policy, uncertainty, provenance, failure, replay, correction, 
and learning behavior.
Sovereignty Customer control over data location, model and provider eligibility, keys, administration, network 
egress, updates, evidence, and operational authority.
Deployment profile Approved topology, artifacts, accelerators, connectivity, providers, capacity, controls, operations, 
and degraded-mode envelope.
Parity evidence Common conformance suite plus profile-specific security, performance, resilience, capacity, update, 
and recovery evidence.
Normative requirements
ID REQUIREMENT VERIFICATION REQUIRED EVIDENCE

correction, and learning contracts.

independent authority, scope,

mechanisms only when the replacement passes task-specific 
semantic, safety, policy, calibration, provenance, performance, 
and replay conformance.

reviewer, and fitness decision.

Security / privacy 
test

task, artifacts, data class, provider, 
deployment, and policy.
AI-71-005
Capacity or accelerator limits SHALL produce explicit queueing, 
lower-throughput approved resolution, partial service, or 
unavailability and SHALL NOT silently weaken quality, safety, 
explanation, or human control.
System test + review
End-to-end result plus named 
independent authority, scope, 
rationale, reservations, and time.

Failure semantics
FAILURE CONDITION REQUIRED BEHAVIOR

update, and deletion boundaries.

72  AI Incident, Continuity, and Governance Closure
AI incidents are managed as failures of a governed sociotechnical system. Response 
contains model and agent behavior, protects people and operations, preserves 
evidence, identifies affected products and decisions, communicates uncertainty, 
restores controlled capability, and converts verified lessons into governed 
improvements without compromising the historical record.
Figure 73. AI Incident, Continuity, and Governance Closure. The diagram organizes ai incident, command, continuity modes, 
closure as one governed AI architecture contract.
AI architecture contract
CONTRACT ELEMENT BINDING DEFINITION
AI incident Observed or credible failure involving harmful, unsafe, unauthorized, misleading, unaccountable, 
unavailable, or uncontrolled AI behavior or dependency.
Command Named incident authority with consequence-based roles for AI, security, data, platform, product, 
legal, domain, communications, and customer operations.
Continuity modes Normal, constrained, human-only, deterministic fallback, read-only, isolated, recovery, and 
unavailable.
Closure Contained, corrected, impact-accounted, notified, restored, monitored, lessons released, and 
independently approved with residual risk explicit.
Normative requirements
ID REQUIREMENT VERIFICATION REQUIRED EVIDENCE
AI-72-001
AI incident detection SHALL create a durable case that binds 
affected tenants, tasks, artifacts, agents, data, tools, products, 
decisions, effects, deployments, time range, evidence, severity, 
and command authority.
Fault / recovery 
exercise
Fault trace, checkpoint, product 
state, effect reconciliation, recovery, 
and accountable closure.
AI-72-002
Containment SHALL support artifact revocation, agent and 
workflow suspension, capability withdrawal, product quarantine, 
warning suppression or annotation, provider isolation, human-
only operation, and evidence preservation.
Fault / recovery 
exercise
Fault trace, checkpoint, product 
state, effect reconciliation, recovery, 
and accountable closure.
AI-72-003
Impact analysis SHALL traverse provenance and dependency 
edges to identify affected outputs, warnings, recommendations, 
decisions, commitments, exports, customers, evaluations, 
datasets, and learned artifacts.
System test + review
End-to-end result plus named 
independent authority, scope, 
rationale, reservations, and time.
AI-72-004
Restoration SHALL require verified cause treatment, regression 
evidence, safety and fitness reassessment, deployment-parity 
validation, operational rehearsal, accountable approval, 
progressive exposure, and enhanced monitoring.
Security / privacy 
test
Signed adversarial result bound to 
task, artifacts, data class, provider, 
deployment, and policy.

Failure semantics
FAILURE CONDITION REQUIRED BEHAVIOR
Scope remains uncertain Apply the broader credible containment boundary and communicate uncertainty explicitly.
Primary AI path unavailable Enter the approved continuity mode; never simulate normal service with ungoverned substitutes.
Closure evidence incomplete Keep the incident and affected conformance state open and restrict restoration or scope 
expansion.
Assurance evidence
• Incident exercises cover harmful output, approval bypass, data leakage, provider compromise, 
agent loop, model withdrawal, drift, and widespread revocation.
• Continuity tests prove human-only and deterministic fallback modes preserve authority, evidence, 
policy, communication, and recovery state.
• Impact fixtures trace seeded defects through products, decisions, commitments, exports, 
evaluations, and learning artifacts.
• Governance review verifies requirement, invariant, engineering, component, agent, model, risk, 
evaluation, and deployment traceability before baseline approval.

AGENT RULE
An agent may prepare a proposal, assessment, forecast, scenario, simulation, explanation, 
warning, or decision package. Canonical admission remains with the owning layer; consequential 
choice and irreversible effect remain with an authorized human.

D  Model Class and Task Catalog
Model classes are interchangeable method families behind stable task and product 
contracts. Approval is never inherited from a class: each exact artifact and composition 
requires scope-specific fitness, safety, rights, sovereignty, and operational evidence.
ID MODEL CLASS PRIMARY TASKS REQUIRED EVALUATION AUTHORITY 
BOUNDARY
MC-001 Large language 
model
Language understanding, generation, 
structured reasoning support
Task correctness, faithfulness, 
calibration, safety, latency
No direct decision or tool 
authority
MC-002 Vision-language 
model
Image, diagram, map, chart, and 
document-image interpretation
Grounding, spatial accuracy, OCR 
fidelity, safety
Policy-filtered images 
and region-level 
evidence
MC-003 Speech and audio 
model
Transcription, diarization, event and 
acoustic classification
Word and speaker error, timing, 
language, noise robustness
Consent, biometric, and 
retention controls
MC-004 OCR and document 
model
Layout, table, handwriting, form, and 
document structure extraction
Field accuracy, reading order, 
coverage, uncertainty
Source image remains 
authoritative evidence
MC-005 Embedding model Semantic representation for retrieval, 
clustering, matching, and novelty
Recall, neighborhood quality, drift, 
privacy
Never substitutes for 
identity, policy, or 
provenance
MC-006 Reranking model Task-aware ordering of retrieved 
candidates
NDCG, recall at cutoff, evidence 
diversity, latency
Runs after authorization; 
ranking is not truth
MC-007 Classifier Governed category, relevance, 
sensitivity, topic, and routing labels
Precision, recall, calibration, 
abstention, fairness
Candidate label until 
owning schema admits
MC-008 NER and span 
extractor
Entity, event, claim, quantity, and 
temporal mention extraction
Span and type F1, novelty, 
multilingual robustness
Cannot create canonical 
identity
MC-009 Relationship 
extraction model
Typed directed relationship candidates 
with supporting spans
Endpoint, type, direction, time, 
evidence accuracy
Graph stewardship 
required for commit
MC-010 Graph model and 
algorithm
Traversal, link prediction, community, 
influence, and graph representation
Path faithfulness, leakage, temporal 
validity, robustness
Canonical graph remains 
source of identity and 
relations
MC-011 Time-series 
forecasting model
Point, interval, quantile, and distribution 
forecasts
Calibration, coverage, sharpness, 
horizon and drift
Exact cutoff and 
population required
MC-012 Probabilistic and 
Bayesian model
Uncertainty-aware inference, updating, 
and risk estimation
Posterior calibration, prior 
sensitivity, identifiability
Assumptions and priors 
explicit
MC-013 Causal model Causal structure, effect estimation, 
counterfactual analysis
Identification, balance, sensitivity, 
transportability
Causal claim requires 
declared assumptions
MC-014 Anomaly and weak-
signal model
Novelty, change-point, outlier, 
convergence, and precursor detection
Detection delay, false alarms, 
stability, lead time
Candidate signal until 
mechanism and 
materiality review
MC-015 Optimization model
Constrained resource, routing, 
scheduling, portfolio, and policy option 
optimization
Feasibility, optimality gap, 
sensitivity, robustness
Objectives and 
constraints human-
approved
MC-016 Simulation model
Discrete event, agent-based, system 
dynamics, Monte Carlo, and domain 
simulation
Validity, reproducibility, sensitivity, 
uncertainty
Synthetic branch 
isolation mandatory
MC-017 Policy or control 
model
Bounded adaptive selection within an 
approved environment
Reward validity, safety, regret, 
stability, off-policy risk
No self-authorized goals 
or external effect
MC-018 Evaluator or judge 
model
Assist scoring of correctness, quality, 
safety, and explanation
Agreement, bias, calibration, 
robustness, contamination
Never sole authority for 
own lineage
MC-019 Safety and content 
model
Detect unsafe, restricted, sensitive, or 
policy-relevant content
Recall at severity, false denial, 
evasion robustness
External policy 
enforcement remains 
authoritative
MC-020 Translation and 
localization model
Cross-language translation and 
terminology-controlled rendering
Meaning preservation, named 
entities, numbers, caveats
Source-language 
evidence and uncertainty 
preserved

restrictive as an operation moves from observation toward consequential and 
irreversible external effect.

execution state. Audience renderings may simplify presentation but cannot change 
meaning, uncertainty, limitations, dissent, or authority.

Constitutional AI architecture matrix
ID INVARIANT BINDING STATEMENT PRIMARY VOLUME 5 
ANCHORS
C-001 Category integrity
The Eye shall be conceived, designed, sold, and operated as a Strategic 
Intelligence Operating System. It shall not be reduced to a chatbot, dashboard, BI 
tool, copilot, or reporting product.
Ch. 1, Ch. 7, Ch. 19
C-002 Closed strategic loop
The product shall implement one continuous operating loop: Observe, 
Understand, Predict, Simulate, Decide, and Learn. No stage may be treated as a 
disconnected feature island.
Ch. 9, Ch. 57, Ch. 2, Ch. 20
C-003 Product boundary Interfaces such as chat, dashboards, reports, and APIs may exist, but they are 
access modes to the operating system rather than the product definition.
Ch. 1, Ch. 7, Ch. 8, Ch. 71, 
Ch. 3, Ch. 21
C-004 Human decision 
sovereignty
A named human authority shall make every final strategic or high-impact decision. 
The system may advise, prepare, and execute approved workflows, but it shall not 
silently assume decision rights.
Ch. 1, Ch. 2, Ch. 3, Ch. 7, Ch. 
11, Ch. 14, Ch. 70, Ch. 72, Ch. 
58, Ch. 59, Ch. 62, Ch. 65, Ch. 
46, Ch. 47, Ch. 51, Ch. 55, Ch. 
31, Ch. 32, Ch. 33, Ch. 34, Ch. 
35, Ch. 36, Ch. 37, Ch. 18, Ch. 
20, Ch. 4, Ch. 22

THE EYE VOLUME 5  /  AI ARCHITECTURE
THE EYE  /  CONTROLLED AI BASELINE V1.0 PAGE 192
ID INVARIANT BINDING STATEMENT PRIMARY VOLUME 5 
ANCHORS
C-005 Explain every 
recommendation
Every material forecast, scenario, simulation result, risk, opportunity, and 
recommendation shall expose evidence, assumptions, uncertainty, lineage, 
alternatives, and accountable system versions.
Ch. 2, Ch. 3, Ch. 4, Ch. 5, Ch. 
6, Ch. 9, Ch. 12, Ch. 66, Ch. 
58, Ch. 60, Ch. 61, Ch. 63, Ch. 
64, Ch. 40, Ch. 42, Ch. 43, Ch. 
44, Ch. 46, Ch. 48, Ch. 49, Ch. 
50, Ch. 51, Ch. 52, Ch. 53, Ch. 
54, Ch. 55, Ch. 56, Ch. 57, Ch. 
32, Ch. 35, Ch. 37, Ch. 16, Ch. 
17, Ch. 18, Ch. 19, Ch. 20, Ch. 
24, Ch. 26, Ch. 27, Ch. 29, Ch. 
23
C-006 Enterprise operating 
standard
The common platform shall satisfy enterprise requirements for governance, 
security, scale, auditability, reliability, and lifecycle management from its first 
production architecture.
Ch. 6, Ch. 24
C-007 Universal core, governed 
specialization
A single domain-neutral core shall serve all customer classes. Domain ontologies, 
models, workflows, agents, and policies may extend the core without forking its 
constitutional semantics.
Ch. 7, Ch. 25
C-008 Ten-layer architecture
The ten named architecture layers are the canonical product structure. Cross-
cutting planes may support them but shall not replace, collapse, or rename them 
without constitutional amendment.
Ch. 1, Ch. 7, Ch. 8, Ch. 26
C-009 Knowledge Graph 
centrality
The Knowledge Graph shall be the connected semantic heart of The Eye and the 
shared context substrate for memory, twins, reasoning, prediction, scenarios, 
simulations, and decisions.
Ch. 12, Ch. 13, Ch. 43, Ch. 44, 
Ch. 45, Ch. 53, Ch. 54, Ch. 23, 
Ch. 24, Ch. 27, Ch. 28, Ch. 9
C-010 Truth-state separation
Observed evidence, extracted claims, inferred relationships, model outputs, 
human judgments, and synthetic scenario data shall remain explicitly 
distinguishable throughout the system.
Ch. 4, Ch. 9, Ch. 12, Ch. 69, 
Ch. 64, Ch. 65, Ch. 41, Ch. 42, 
Ch. 43, Ch. 44, Ch. 46, Ch. 19, 
Ch. 20, Ch. 27, Ch. 29, Ch. 10, 
Ch. 28
C-011 Temporal truth
Material facts and relationships shall preserve event time, observation time, 
validity time, and system-record time so that past world states can be 
reconstructed without hindsight contamination.
Ch. 6, Ch. 9, Ch. 12, Ch. 13, 
Ch. 39, Ch. 41, Ch. 42, Ch. 43, 
Ch. 44, Ch. 45, Ch. 55, Ch. 31, 
Ch. 34, Ch. 38, Ch. 19, Ch. 23, 
Ch. 24, Ch. 25, Ch. 26, Ch. 27, 
Ch. 29, Ch. 11
C-012 End-to-end provenance
Every material intelligence object shall carry source, chain of custody, 
transformation history, ownership, rights, quality, and version lineage back to its 
evidence.
Ch. 4, Ch. 6, Ch. 7, Ch. 9, Ch. 
10, Ch. 12, Ch. 60, Ch. 64, Ch. 
39, Ch. 40, Ch. 41, Ch. 42, Ch. 
43, Ch. 44, Ch. 45, Ch. 46, Ch. 
53, Ch. 54, Ch. 56, Ch. 30, Ch. 
31, Ch. 35, Ch. 38, Ch. 15, Ch. 
17, Ch. 18, Ch. 19, Ch. 20, Ch. 
23, Ch. 24, Ch. 27, Ch. 29
C-013 Observation breadth The World Observation Layer shall support the full canonical source universe and 
allow governed custom sources without architectural rework.
Ch. 67, Ch. 71, Ch. 60, Ch. 61, 
Ch. 39, Ch. 40, Ch. 54, Ch. 13, 
Ch. 31
C-014 Freshness and quality 
contracts
Sources, derived data, twins, models, and intelligence products shall declare 
measurable freshness, completeness, quality, and expiry expectations.
Ch. 61, Ch. 39, Ch. 40, Ch. 41, 
Ch. 19, Ch. 22, Ch. 23, Ch. 14, 
Ch. 32
C-015 Durable institutional 
memory
The Eye shall preserve institutional context, decisions, assumptions, evidence, 
outcomes, and lessons as durable memory, subject to lawful retention, deletion, 
and customer policy.
Ch. 12, Ch. 13, Ch. 61, Ch. 42, 
Ch. 23, Ch. 25, Ch. 15, Ch. 33
C-016 Memory governance
Memory shall be permission-aware, tenant-isolated, versioned, retrievable, 
correctable, and protected from unverified model output becoming accepted 
organizational truth.
Ch. 69, Ch. 61, Ch. 25, Ch. 16, 
Ch. 34
C-017 Strategy Graph
Objectives, hypotheses, decisions, initiatives, capabilities, resources, indicators, 
risks, opportunities, and outcomes shall be connected in a governed Strategy 
Graph anchored to the Knowledge Graph.
Ch. 13, Ch. 44, Ch. 45, Ch. 51, 
Ch. 52, Ch. 24, Ch. 17, Ch. 35
C-018 Grounded Digital Twins
Every Digital Twin shall be grounded in evidence, graph entities, temporal state, 
declared assumptions, and calibrated models; it shall never present synthetic state 
as observed fact.
Ch. 13, Ch. 50, Ch. 53, Ch. 28, 
Ch. 18, Ch. 36
C-019 Twin versionability Digital Twins shall support time travel, branching, comparison, replay, 
reconciliation, and versioned evolution across actual and simulated states.
Ch. 13, Ch. 63, Ch. 49, Ch. 50, 
Ch. 28, Ch. 19, Ch. 37
C-020 Canonical prediction 
horizons
The Prediction Engine shall support 30-day, 90-day, 180-day, 1-year, 3-year, and 
5-year horizons with horizon-appropriate methods and uncertainty.
Ch. 48, Ch. 49, Ch. 52, Ch. 57, 
Ch. 26, Ch. 28, Ch. 20, Ch. 38
C-021 Probabilistic forecasting
Forecasts shall communicate distributions, confidence, calibration, drivers, 
sensitivity, and failure conditions rather than false precision or unsupported 
certainty.
Ch. 4, Ch. 5, Ch. 66, Ch. 68, 
Ch. 63, Ch. 46, Ch. 48, Ch. 49, 
Ch. 22, Ch. 26, Ch. 21, Ch. 39
C-022 Scenario plurality
Scenario Intelligence shall maintain multiple coherent futures, including baseline, 
upside, downside, disruption, and user-defined alternatives; no single forecast 
shall masquerade as destiny.
Ch. 66, Ch. 68, Ch. 63, Ch. 46, 
Ch. 49, Ch. 50, Ch. 52, Ch. 53, 
Ch. 54, Ch. 57, Ch. 26, Ch. 28, 
Ch. 22, Ch. 40

C-023 Reproducible simulation
Every simulation shall declare initial state, assumptions, interventions, model 
versions, constraints, random seeds where applicable, outputs, and sensitivity so 
results can be reproduced and challenged.
Ch. 68, Ch. 63, Ch. 50, Ch. 26, 
Ch. 28, Ch. 23, Ch. 41
C-024 Decision completeness
Decision Intelligence shall compare viable options against explicit objectives, 
constraints, risks, opportunities, trade-offs, second-order effects, reversibility, and 
information value.
Ch. 3, Ch. 4, Ch. 67, Ch. 58, 
Ch. 59, Ch. 62, Ch. 63, Ch. 64, 
Ch. 46, Ch. 49, Ch. 50, Ch. 51, 
Ch. 32, Ch. 37, Ch. 19, Ch. 24, 
Ch. 42
C-025 Decision Replay
The Eye shall preserve the state of knowledge available at decision time and 
support replay against later evidence and outcomes without rewriting the historical 
record.
Ch. 3, Ch. 6, Ch. 9, Ch. 69, 
Ch. 70, Ch. 72, Ch. 59, Ch. 64, 
Ch. 65, Ch. 48, Ch. 50, Ch. 51, 
Ch. 56, Ch. 31, Ch. 34, Ch. 37, 
Ch. 38, Ch. 25, Ch. 26, Ch. 28, 
Ch. 29, Ch. 43
C-026 Objective alignment
Recommendations shall be evaluated against declared objectives, stakeholder 
obligations, risk appetite, policy, and strategic constraints rather than an opaque 
global score.
Ch. 3, Ch. 59, Ch. 65, Ch. 49, 
Ch. 51, Ch. 52, Ch. 26, Ch. 44
C-027 Executive Operating 
System
The executive surface shall organize strategic attention, decisions, scenarios, 
evidence, objectives, commitments, and outcomes. It shall not degrade into a 
passive dashboard collection.
Ch. 67, Ch. 71, Ch. 59, Ch. 60, 
Ch. 61, Ch. 62, Ch. 51, Ch. 56, 
Ch. 37, Ch. 27, Ch. 45
C-028 Bounded agent authority
Every agent shall have a declared purpose, tools, data scope, permissions, 
budgets, escalation rules, prohibited actions, evaluation suite, and accountable 
owner.
Ch. 1, Ch. 2, Ch. 3, Ch. 5, Ch. 
8, Ch. 10, Ch. 11, Ch. 14, Ch. 
66, Ch. 67, Ch. 68, Ch. 69, Ch. 
70, Ch. 72, Ch. 58, Ch. 59, Ch. 
60, Ch. 62, Ch. 65, Ch. 47, Ch. 
55, Ch. 30, Ch. 31, Ch. 32, Ch. 
33, Ch. 34, Ch. 35, Ch. 36, Ch. 
38, Ch. 16, Ch. 18, Ch. 20, Ch. 
28, Ch. 46
C-029 Supervised orchestration
Planner, Supervisor, and Workflow agents shall enforce policy, separation of 
duties, checkpoints, timeouts, retries, human gates, and complete execution 
traces across multi-agent work.
Ch. 3, Ch. 11, Ch. 67, Ch. 72, 
Ch. 58, Ch. 59, Ch. 62, Ch. 30, 
Ch. 31, Ch. 32, Ch. 33, Ch. 34, 
Ch. 35, Ch. 36, Ch. 38, Ch. 29, 
Ch. 47
C-030 Model pluralism
The platform shall support multiple models and reasoning approaches behind 
governed interfaces so customers retain control over cost, sovereignty, 
performance, and vendor dependence.

O  AI Architecture Decision Baseline
These decisions freeze the governing AI architecture beneath Volumes 0, 3, and 4. 
Implementations may change technologies and providers behind them but cannot treat 
a local exception as authority to change their semantics.
ADR DECISION BINDING CONSEQUENCE IMPLEMENTATION OBLIGATION
AI-ADR-001 AI remains subordinate to the ten 
canonical layers
No AI-owned parallel product or truth 
plane
Implement through owned components, 
versioned contracts, external controls, 
conformance evidence, release state, 
monitoring, rollback, and audit.

delegable

conformance evidence, release state, 
monitoring, rollback, and audit.

independent

P  AI Glossary
These terms are canonical for models, agents, context, evaluation, safety, governance, 
delivery, and AI operations. Domain specifications may add narrower terms but may not 
redefine the concepts below.
TERM AI ARCHITECTURE DEFINITION
Abstention Explicit refusal to produce or admit a result because fitness, evidence, policy, calibration, or integrity 
is insufficient.
Agent Versioned execution package that performs a bounded role under task, context, models, tools, policy, 
supervision, budgets, and stop conditions.
Agent package Immutable agent manifest, instructions, code, schemas, dependencies, evaluation, controls, and 
release metadata.
Agent trajectory Ordered plan, messages, context, model calls, tools, checkpoints, reviews, outputs, and state 
transitions of one execution.
AI artifact Versioned model, adapter, prompt, agent, evaluator, dataset, calibrator, rule, policy proposal, or 
learned configuration.
AI product Canonical intelligence object produced or assisted by AI and admitted through its owning layer.
AI release Dependency-closed approved configuration of all behavior-affecting AI artifacts and controls.
Calibration Measured relationship between predicted confidence or probability and observed frequency for a 
declared population.
Capability grant Short-lived least-privilege authorization for an exact tool, operation, target, purpose, limit, identity, and 
time.
Consequence class Control class derived from potential harm, scale, irreversibility, vulnerability, and reachable effects of 
a precise use.
Context Authorized, temporal, provenance-bearing information and control boundary supplied to an AI task.
Context manifest Integrity-bound record of sources, retrieval, time, graph, twins, memory, evidence, instructions, policy, 
quality, and omissions.
Contest Durable challenge to a material AI claim, source, method, result, recommendation, or policy 
application.
Decision sovereignty Principle that a named authorized human retains final authority for consequential institutional 
decisions.
Dissent Preserved material disagreement among evidence, models, agents, experts, or stakeholders.
Drift Material change in data, concepts, populations, behavior, calibration, tools, policy, providers, 
workload, or outcomes.
Evaluator Governed method or reviewer that measures an exact AI configuration against a declared evaluation 
claim.
Evidence binding Resolvable connection from a claim or field to exact supporting source or object state and 
transformation lineage.
Execution envelope Typed identity, authority, task, context, artifact, budget, tool, policy, evidence, and lifecycle contract 
for a run.
Experience memory Approved reusable task patterns and lessons released through governance, distinct from raw 
execution history.
Explainability Ability to account faithfully for evidence, methods, assumptions, uncertainty, alternatives, controls, 
and limitations.
Explanation product Versioned structured object that binds an AI result to its decision-relevant evidence and execution 
state.
Fitness Scope-specific determination that an exact configuration is suitable for a task, population, 
consequence, deployment, and time.
Grounding Binding model or agent work to governed entities, relationships, memory, graph, twins, time, 
evidence, and truth states.
Human gate Durable boundary at which a named authorized human approves, rejects, changes, abstains, or 
escalates an exact act.
=== PAGE 198 ===
THE EYE VOLUME 5  /  AI ARCHITECTURE
THE EYE  /  CONTROLLED AI BASELINE V1.0 PAGE 198
TERM AI ARCHITECTURE DEFINITION
Hybrid retrieval Governed combination of lexical, semantic, graph, temporal, geospatial, structured, and memory 
retrieval.
Inference Computation that produces a probabilistic, assessed, or generated result without independently 
establishing canonical truth.
Instruction authority Ordered trust hierarchy separating controlling artifacts and approved directions from untrusted 
content.
Model adapter Provider-specific binding that translates a canonical task contract while preserving semantics and 
controls.
Model gateway Governed interface that resolves, invokes, observes, and normalizes approved model artifacts.
Model pluralism Use of replaceable model and method families behind stable task and product contracts.
Operating envelope Evaluated bounds of task, data, population, quality, uncertainty, safety, deployment, and operational 
conditions.
Partial result Output that explicitly identifies completed work, missing dependencies, affected claims, and permitted 
downstream use.
Policy guard Advisory agent that detects possible control conflicts while authoritative enforcement remains 
external.
Product admission Owning-layer validation and commit that converts a proposal into governed canonical state.
Prompt artifact Signed versioned instruction or template resolved for a precise task, model, policy, and release.
Provider abstraction Isolation of provider protocols and semantics behind canonical model and tool interfaces.
Red teaming Authorized adversarial testing of the complete AI system, controls, people, and operational response.
Replay Reconstruction of an execution or decision using its exact historical information, artifacts, controls, 
and authority.
Residual risk Risk remaining after verified controls, accepted by a named authority for a bounded scope and time.
Risk acceptance Explicit time-bounded accountable decision to operate with stated residual risk and monitoring.
Run memory Tenant- and execution-scoped plans, messages, tools, checkpoints, intermediate products, and 
reviews.
Safety case Structured argument connecting hazards, controls, assumptions, evidence, monitoring, residual risk, 
and release approval.
Side effect Material change outside analytical execution, including communication, commitment, financial, cyber, 
physical, or operational action.
Strategic Memory Connected governed record of strategic evidence, assumptions, objectives, decisions, commitments, 
outcomes, and lessons.
Structured output Typed model or agent result validated against canonical schema and semantic admission rules.
Supervisor External runtime authority that admits, constrains, monitors, suspends, recovers, and terminates 
agent execution.
Synthetic branch Scenario or simulated state isolated from observed and estimated canonical state.
Task contract Provider-independent definition of input, output, method constraints, consequence, controls, quality, 
and failure behavior.
Tool Registered typed capability adapter through which an agent reads, computes, proposes, or requests 
an effect.
Tool authority Exact permitted operation, target, purpose, parameter, limit, identity, time, and human-gate boundary.
Truth state Observed, claimed, inferred, assessed, forecast, scenario, simulated, disputed, corrected, or 
withdrawn status.
Uncertainty Explicit representation of randomness, lack of knowledge, ambiguity, disagreement, model limits, and 
incompleteness.
Weak signal Low-strength but potentially meaningful evidence of emerging change whose mechanism and 
materiality remain under assessment.
Workflow Durable versioned coordination of tasks, waits, gates, retries, compensation, evidence, and terminal 
state.

THE EYE VOLUME 5  /  AI ARCHITECTURE
THE EYE  /  CONTROLLED AI BASELINE V1.0 PAGE 199
Baseline closure
Volume 5 closes when every AI behavior can identify its controlling constitutional invariant, canonical 
layer, engineering owner, task and product contract, model and agent artifacts, context and evidence 
boundary, consequence class, policy and capability controls, human gate, uncertainty, explanation, 
evaluation, safety case, release, monitoring, deployment-parity evidence, incident path, learning path, 
and retirement state.
END STATE
The Eye operates here as one governed Strategic Intelligence Operating System: continuously 
observing the world, constructing evidence-bound understanding, remembering institutional truth, 
connecting knowledge, reconciling living twins, forecasting and simulating possible futures, 
preparing explainable decision packages for named humans, governing effects and commitments, 
measuring outcomes, and learning only through accountable release.
